--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 per stationary robot → robots should MOVE before switch
- After switch time: score += 1 per moving robot → robots should STOP after switch
- Switch signal: a wall block lights up GREEN
- So the ideal behavior: move (explore) until a green object is perceived, then stop

**Key insight:**
- Robots should explore/move before the green wall appears
- When a robot perceives GREEN (color 2), it should stop
- The floor is uniformly GRAY, so floor color isn't useful
- Neighbor count could be somewhat useful but not the primary signal

**State design:**
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when GREEN is perceived
- State 1: Stop → maybe transition back if no green perceived (but risky)

The simplest effective design is:
- 2 states: Explore → Stop on perceiving green; Stop stays stopped
- The green wall lights up at switch time, so detecting it is the key trigger

Let me design varied machines:

1. **Best guess - 2 state**: Explore with high turn rate → Stop on C=7 (green, color=2), high probability
2. Variants with different probabilities
3. 3-state variants with attraction as intermediate
4. GoToColor green then stop
5. Variants with multiple transitions

For color IDs: I need to check which color is GREEN. The problem says "lights up GREEN". In AutoMoDe color IDs: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So green = 2.

Let me create 14 machines:

**FSM1 (base):** 2 states: Explore→Stop on green(p=0.9), Stop→Explore on no-green (p=0.01)
**FSM2:** Similar but p=1.0 for green detection
**FSM3:** 3 states with GoToColor intermediate
**FSM4-14:** Various explorations

But with 2 states, both transitions from S0 go to S1 (D=0, since there's only one other state). So multiple transitions from S0 always go to S1. That's fine, it just means multiple firing conditions.

- S0: Explore(rwm=15), LED=0. 2 transitions: green(p=0.95)→S1, random(p=0.001)→S1
- S1: Stop, LED=0. 1 transition: random(p=0.001)→S0

**Machine 6:** 3-state: Explore → GoToColor → Stop
- S0: Explore(rwm=10). 1 trans: green(p=1.0) → D=0 → S1
- S1: GoToColor(green). 1 trans: neighbors>3(p=3,w=10) → D=1 → S2
- S2: Stop. 1 trans: random(p=0.001) → D=0 → S0

**Machine 7:** 3-state: Explore → Stop → (rare back)
- S0: Explore(rwm=20). 1 trans: green(p=1.0) → D=0 → S1
- S1: Stop. 2 trans: random(p=0.005)→D=0→S0, green(p=0.1)→D=1→S2 (to explore more?)
- S2: Explore(rwm=10). 1 trans: green(p=1.0) → D=0 → S0 (goes back to explore... hmm)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed well. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and the mission structure.

## Reasoning

**Mission recap:**
- Before switch: penalty for stationary robots → should MOVE
- After switch: penalty for moving robots → should STOP
- Switch signal: a GREEN wall block lights up
- Key behavior: Explore until green is perceived, then Stop permanently

**Design principles:**
- Primary trigger: C=7 (perceive green, color=2) with high probability → transition to Stop
- Stop state should be "sticky" (very low probability of leaving)
- Exploration should cover the arena well so robots can detect the green wall
- The green wall is on the arena boundary, so robots need to spread out to see it

**Key considerations:**
- Not all robots may directly see the green wall, but those that do should stop and possibly signal via LEDs
- Neighbor-based conditions could help propagate the "stop" signal
- Multiple transitions from Explore state give robustness

I'll generate 14 machines covering:
- Simple 2-state (Explore→Stop on green)
- 2-state with neighbor propagation  
- 3-state with GoToColor intermediate
- Variants with different turning rates and probabilities
- LED signaling to help non-seeing robots

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no score feedback from the rounds. I'll reason carefully from first principles and generate a diverse, well-structured population.

## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:**
- Before switch (~400-600 steps): penalty for stationary → MOVE
- After switch: penalty for moving → STOP
- Signal: GREEN wall lights up at switch time
- Optimal: explore freely, then stop immediately when green is perceived

**Why simple 2-state might be suboptimal:**
- Not every robot can see the green wall directly (range-and-bearing is 0.5m, camera has limited FOV)
- Robots near the center may never see the wall
- Need a propagation mechanism: robots that stop could signal others via LEDs

**LED propagation strategy:**
- Exploring robots: LED=yellow (color 4)
- Stopped robots (saw green): LED=cyan (color 6)
- Other exploring robots: if they see cyan (color 6) neighbors → also stop
- This propagates the "stop" signal through the swarm

**Key design:**
- State 0: Explore (LED=yellow=4) → stop on: (a) see green wall (C=7, color=2), or (b) see cyan robot (C=7, color=6)
- State 1: Stop (LED=cyan=6) → very rarely leave

This LED-propagation design should be much better than purely individual sensing.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no explicit score feedback. Let me reason more carefully about what might be going wrong and try more diverse approaches.

## Reasoning

**Re-examining the mission carefully:**

The score before switch = sum of stationary robots per step. After switch = sum of moving robots per step.

With 20 robots and switch at ~500 steps, 1200 total:
- Ideal score ≈ 0 (all moving before switch) + 0 (all stopped after switch) = 0
- Worst case: all stop entire run OR all move entire run ≈ 20×1200 = 24000

**What might be failing:**
1. The green signal might not be visible to all robots (camera range/FOV limited)
2. LED propagation might work but cyan (color 6) might conflict with green (color 2) detection
3. The C=7 condition (perceive color) - I need to verify: does it detect wall LEDs AND robot LEDs? Yes, it should detect any colored object.
4. Maybe the stopping probability on C=7 should be lower to avoid false positives before switch

**Critical insight I may have missed:**
- Before switch, if a robot accidentally sees green from another robot's LED or artifact, it stops too early → huge penalty
- The cyan propagation could cause premature stopping if any robot has cyan LED early
- Better: use NO colored LEDs in the explore state, only light up when truly stopped

**Revised strategy:**
- Keep LEDs off (0) during exploration
- When green wall seen → stop AND light up magenta (5)
- Others: if they see magenta (5) → also stop and light up magenta
- This creates a clean cascade with no false positives

**Also trying:** neighbor-count based propagation - when many neighbors are stopped (clustered), stop too. But this is harder to detect directly.

**Also trying:** Pure individual sensing without propagation - simplest and most robust.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still have no score feedback. Let me reason more carefully about potential failure modes and try fundamentally different approaches.

## Reasoning

**What might be going wrong with my current designs:**

1. **C=7 (perceive color) might detect robot LEDs AND wall colors** — if exploring robots have colored LEDs, they might trigger false stops in other robots before the green wall appears. My "magenta propagation" designs could cause premature stopping.

2. **The green wall is color 2** — but what if the C=7 condition fires on the green wall even BEFORE the switch? The wall is always there, just unlit. If it emits no light before switch, then C=7 for green=2 should only fire after switch. This is the correct assumption.

3. **Maybe the core issue is that robots in the center never see the wall** — the arena is closed, robots start near center, camera range may not reach the wall. If robots never see green, they never stop.

4. **Pure individual detection is actually the right strategy** — robots need to explore outward toward walls to see the green signal. Higher repulsion from center might help spread robots.

5. **Maybe I should try the OPPOSITE baseline** — start stopped, switch to moving when green appears (wrong direction for score), no... wait.

Actually wait: before switch, stationary robots HURT the score. After switch, moving robots HURT. So:
- Phase 1: robots should move → Explore state
- Phase 2: robots should stop → Stop state
- Trigger: green wall appearing

**New hypothesis: maybe simpler is actually better.** Let me try machines that start with Stop and transition to Explore early, then stop on green. No — robots should be moving before switch.

**Let me try completely different topologies:**
- What if robots start stopped (to avoid penalty if switch is very early?). No, switch is 400-600, so ~500 steps moving then ~700 stopped is optimal.
- What about using the neighbor count as a stop signal propagation?

**Most important insight I've been ignoring:** With 20 robots spread in arena of maybe 2-3m diameter, and camera range limited, wall detection might just work for robots near edges. The key is to have robots SPREAD OUT and move toward walls.

Let me try Repulsion-based spreading + green detection, and also GoAwayColor(green) before switch to keep away from green wall (so they don't accidentally see it?). No, that's counterproductive.

**Simplest robust design:** Just explore with high randomness, stop on green. The propagation via LEDs adds value only if it doesn't cause false positives.

**New approach: two-phase with time-based fallback**
- Use random probability transitions as a crude timer
- Start exploring, with a very small chance per step to stop (covering late-switch cases)
- But primarily stop on green

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I have been running 6 rounds without any score feedback shown to me. Let me fundamentally reconsider the problem.

## Reasoning

**Deep reconsideration of the mission:**

The scoring is: before switch → penalty per stationary robot; after switch → penalty per moving robot. The green wall lights up AT the switch time.

**Critical question: Can robots actually perceive the green wall?**
- The problem says robots perceive via range-and-bearing (0.5m range) for neighbors
- They also have a **camera** for colored objects - this is what C=7 uses
- The green wall is a wall block that "lights up GREEN" - this should be visible to the camera
- But wall distance may exceed camera range in some arenas

**Alternative interpretation:** Maybe the camera in e-puck ARGoS3 only detects other robots' LEDs, not wall colors. If that's the case, C=7 for green=2 would NEVER fire (since no robot has green LEDs in my designs), and all my designs would just be "Explore forever then stop with p=0.001."

**If C=7 detects only robot LEDs:** Then to propagate green signal, a robot that somehow knows the switch happened must light up green (color 2), and others detect that. But how does the first robot know?

**Alternative signals:**
- The floor is GRAY uniformly → C=1 (gray floor) always fires, useless as a switch
- Neighbor count changes? If robots stop, their neighbors decrease?
- No direct perception of the switch time

**Revised strategy if C=7 detects walls:** My designs should work. Maybe the issue is robots aren't reaching the wall.

**Revised strategy if C=7 only detects robot LEDs:** Need a completely different approach. Use stochastic timing: robots explore for ~500 steps then stop. With p=0.002/step, expected time to transition = 500 steps. This matches the switch time distribution!

**Stochastic timer approach:**
- Explore state: transition to Stop with p=0.002 per step (expected 500 steps)
- Stop state: stay stopped (very low exit probability)
- This is a "blind" strategy but may actually work well given the switch time distribution

**Hybrid approach:**
- Explore with p_stop ≈ 0.002 per step AND stop on green (C=7)
- This covers both cases

The expected switch time is 500 steps. p=0.002 gives E[time]=500. p=0.0015 gives E[time]=667, p=0.003 gives E[time]=333. The switch is uniform[400,600] so mean=500.

Let me try a range of stochastic timer probabilities plus green detection:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I have been operating blind for 7 rounds. Let me completely rethink from scratch.

## Deep Reasoning

**The core problem with all my designs:**

I've been assuming robots can detect the green wall via C=7. But I realize I need to think about what the e-puck camera actually detects in ARGoS3 AutoMoDe. The camera detects **colored blobs** - which includes both robot LEDs AND colored objects in the environment (like lit wall segments). So C=7 with color=2 (green) SHOULD fire when the green wall lights up.

**But why might it still fail?**
- The green wall is ONE block out of many - it occupies a small angular segment
- Robots must be facing roughly toward that wall to detect it
- A robot exploring randomly will eventually face the green wall... but the camera detection probability p in C=7 adds another layer of randomness

**The stochastic timer (p≈0.002) approach should work as a fallback.** Expected stop time = 500 steps = mean switch time. But variance is huge - some robots stop at 100 steps (penalty before switch) and some at 900 (penalty after switch).

**What if I combine both strategies optimally?**
- Use green detection (C=7) as primary signal
- Use stochastic timer as secondary
- But the timer probability needs careful tuning

**Actually, let me reconsider the optimal timer probability:**
- Switch uniform[400,600], mean=500
- After switch, robots that are still moving cost points
- Before switch, robots that stopped cost points
- The optimal individual strategy: stop exactly at step 500
- With geometric distribution: p=0.002 → E[stop]=500, but high variance
- Better: use a LOWER p so most robots stay moving until late, then stop near 500

**Key math:** If p is the per-step stop probability, the expected score contribution from one robot is:
- Expected steps stationary before switch + expected steps moving after switch

This is minimized when the robot stops right at the switch time. The geometric distribution with p=1/500 has mode at step 1, which is terrible. A better approach might be to use TWO transitions: one that rarely fires early (p very small) and the green wall detection.

**Most important insight I've been missing:** Maybe I should try machines where the STOP state is the INITIAL state, and robots transition to EXPLORE only briefly, then back. No - that's worse because before switch we want movement.

**Let me try pure timer machines with very carefully tuned probabilities, plus green detection:**

The switch is uniform[400,600]. After switch lasts 1200-T steps where T~U[400,600].

For a robot that stops at step S:
- If S < T (stopped too early): penalty = S steps stationary before switch... wait no. Before switch, penalty is for STATIONARY robots. So stopping early (S<T) means (T-S) steps of stationary penalty before switch, plus 0 after.
- If S > T (still moving after switch): penalty = (S-T) steps moving after switch, plus (1200-S) steps... wait no. After S the robot is stopped, so no penalty after S. Before T the robot moves (good). Between T and S the robot moves (bad).

So total penalty for one robot stopping at step S:
- If S ≤ T: (T - S) [stationary before switch] → minimize by maximizing S up to T
- If S > T: (S - T) [moving after switch] → minimize by minimizing S down to T

Optimal: S = T exactly. Since T~U[400,600], the best fixed S is 500.

But with geometric stopping: P(stop by step t) = 1-(1-p)^t. The expected penalty is complex, but the **mode** of stopping should be near 500.

With p=0.002: geometric has no useful mode (decreasing). The median stop time is ln(0.5)/ln(0.998) ≈ 346 steps. Too early!

To get median≈500: p = 1-0.5^(1/500) ≈ 0.00139. Let's use p=0.0014.

**But with green detection as well:** Once green fires (at T≈500), robots stop immediately. The random timer is just a backup for robots that never see green.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I have been operating completely blind for 8 rounds with no score feedback. Let me take a completely fresh perspective.

## Deep Reasoning

**What I know for certain:**
- Before switch (~400-600 steps): penalty per stationary robot → must MOVE
- After switch: penalty per moving robot → must STOP  
- Green wall lights up at switch time
- Floor is uniformly GRAY
- 20 robots, 1200 steps total

**What I've tried and likely failed at:**
- Pure green detection (C=7, color=2)
- LED propagation (cyan/magenta)
- Stochastic timers (p≈0.0014-0.002)
- Combinations of all above

**What I haven't tried seriously:**
1. **Starting in STOP state** - wait, before switch robots should move, so starting stopped is bad
2. **Very different timer values** - what if the optimal p is very different from what I calculated?
3. **Neighbor-based coordination** - if stopped robots cluster, moving robots detect few neighbors and stop too
4. **The "always move" baseline** - score = 20 × (1200-T) ≈ 20×700 = 14000 for post-switch phase only
5. **The "always stop" baseline** - score = 20 × T ≈ 20×500 = 10000 for pre-switch phase only

**Critical insight:** Always-stop scores ~10000, always-move scores ~14000. A perfect solution scores ~0. My designs should beat 10000 easily if green detection works. If scores are near 10000-14000, my designs aren't working at all.

**New hypothesis: Maybe the issue is the Stop state has a non-zero exit probability that causes robots to re-enter exploration after stopping, leading to accumulated post-switch penalties.**

With p=0.001 exit from Stop, expected time in Stop = 1000 steps. If a robot stops at step 500 and re-explores at step 1500 (beyond run), fine. But if it re-explores at step 700 (200 steps after switch), that's 500 extra penalty steps.

**Solution: Make Stop state truly absorbing - use p=0.0 exit probability.**

But p=0.0 might not be allowed... actually looking at the spec: `--p<I>x<K> real in [0..1]`. So p=0.0 should work! But does the simulator allow it? Let me use p=0.0 to make Stop truly absorbing.

Wait - the rule says every state must have n>=1 transitions. But if p=0.0, the transition never fires. That's effectively absorbing. Let me use p=0.0.

**Also new insight:** What if I try machines where the initial state is Stop and transition to Explore happens with some probability, then transition back to Stop on green? This is weird but: if switch happens early (400), robots in Stop get penalized for 400 steps, then must move... No this is bad.

**Most promising new approach:** Pure stochastic timer with TRULY absorbing stop, varied probabilities, and green detection. Also try having Stop state with p=0.0 (truly absorbing).

**Also trying:** What if the green wall detection color is NOT 2? Let me reconsider. The problem says "lights up GREEN." In AutoMoDe color coding: the problem statement says `--clr<I> one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}` with green=2. But wait - maybe in the actual ARGoS3 LED color encoding used by AutoMoDe, colors are different. Let me try color=3 (which might be green in some encodings) as well.

**Also trying:** GoToColor with color=2 as the exploration behavior - this actively seeks the green wall!

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I now have a critical piece of feedback: `--cle` (LED color) only allows values {0, 4, 5, 6} — NOT 1, 2, or 3. I was using `--cle1 2` (green LED) in several machines, which crashed the simulator. This explains why some machines failed.

**Valid LED colors: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan**

So I cannot have a robot emit GREEN light. The green signal is ONLY from the wall. This means:
- LED propagation must use magenta (5) or cyan (6) as the "I stopped" signal
- Robots detecting magenta/cyan from neighbors → also stop

**Also reviewing p=0.0:** The spec says `real in [0..1]` so p=0.0 should be valid, making Stop truly absorbing. I'll keep using this.

**Best strategy for this final round:**
1. **Elitism:** Keep the best-looking designs (truly absorbing Stop, green detection + timer)
2. **Fix the LED color bug:** Replace any cle=2 with cle=5 (magenta) for the stopped state
3. **Propagation:** Exploring robots (LED=0/off) → stop on green wall (C=7,l=2) OR see magenta robot (C=7,l=5) → stop and emit magenta
4. **Timer backup:** p≈0.0014 per step as fallback
5. **Varied turning rates and timer probabilities**

The magenta propagation design:
- State 0: Explore (LED=off). Transitions: see green wall → Stop; see magenta robot → Stop; timer → Stop
- State 1: Stop (LED=magenta). Truly absorbing (p=0.0)

This should create a cascade: first robot near green wall stops + emits magenta → neighbors see magenta → stop too → cascade spreads.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
